Nonlocal games with noisy maximally entangled states are decidable
Minglong Qin, Penghui Yao

Abstract
This paper considers a special class of nonlocal games ( G , ψ ) (G,\psi) , where G G is a two-player one-round game, and ψ \psi is a bipartite state independent of G G . In the game ( G , ψ ) \left(G,\psi\right) , the players are allowed to share arbitrarily many copies of ψ \psi . The value of the game ( G , ψ ) (G,\psi) , denoted by ω ∗ ​ ( G , ψ ) \omega^{*}(G,\psi) , is the supremum of the winning probability that the players can achieve with arbitrarily many copies of preshared states ψ \psi . For a noisy maximally entangled state ψ \psi , a two-player one-round game G G and an arbitrarily small precision ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 , this paper proves an upper bound on the number of copies of ψ \psi for the players to win the game with a probability ϵ \epsilon close to ω ∗ ​ ( G , ψ ) \omega^{*}(G,\psi) . A noisy maximally entangled state is a two-qudit state with both marginals being completely mixed states and the maximal correlation being less than 1 1 . In particular, it includes ( 1 − ϵ ) | Ψ m ⟩ ⟨ Ψ m | + ϵ 𝟙 m m ⊗ 𝟙 m m (1-\epsilon)\left|\Psi_{m}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\Psi_{m}\right|+\epsilon\frac{\mathds{1}_{m}}{m}\otimes\frac{\mathds{1}_{m}}{m} for ϵ > 0 \epsilon>0 , where | Ψ m ⟩ = 1 m ∑ i = 0 m − 1 | m , m ⟩ \left|\Psi_{m}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\left|m,m\right\rangle is an m m -dimensional maximally entangled state. Hence, it is feasible to approximately compute ω ∗ ​ ( G , ψ ) \omega^{*}\left(G,\psi\right) to an arbitrarily precision. Recently, a breakthrough result by Ji, Natarajan, Vidick, Wright and Yuen showed that it is undecidable to approximate the values of nonlocal games to a constant precision, when the players preshare arbitrarily many copies of perfect maximally entangled states, which implies that MIP ∗ = RE \mathrm{MIP}^{*}=\mathrm{RE} . In contrast, our result implies the hardness of approximating nonlocal games collapses when the preshared maximally entangled states are noisy. The paper develops a theory of Fourier analysis on matrix spaces by extending a number of techniques in Boolean analysis and Hermitian analysis to matrix spaces. We establish a series of new techniques, such as a quantum invariance principle and a hypercontractive inequality for random operators, which we believe have further applications.



1
Introduction

Interactive proof systems
are a fundamental concept to the theory of computing. It was first proposed by Babai
[
5
]
and Goldwasser, Micali and Rackoff
[
33
]
, and later extended to the multiprover setting in
[
12
]
. The study of interactive proof systems is at the heart of the theory of computing, including the elegant characterizations
\mathrm{IP}=\mathrm{PSPACE}

[
74
,
75
]
for single-prover interactive proof systems and
\mathrm{MIP}=\mathrm{NEXP}

[
6
]
for multiprover interactive proof systems. The latter result further led to the celebrated PCP theorem
[
2
,
3
]
.

The study on the power of interactive proof systems in the context of quantum computing also has a rich history. The model of single-prover quantum interactive proof systems was first studied by Watrous
[
82
]
, followed by a series of works
[
57
,
59
,
34
,
44
]
, which finally led to the seminal result
\mathrm{QIP}=\mathrm{PSPACE}

[
43
]
. Quantum multiprover interactive proof systems are more complicated. A key assumption on the classical multiprover interactive proof systems is that the provers are not allowed to communicate, which means that their only resource in common is the shared randomness. In quantum multiprover interactive proof systems, this assumption is relaxed and the provers are allowed to share
entanglement
, and the corresponding complexity class is
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}

[
19
]
. Surprisingly, understanding the power of
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
turns out to be extremely difficult. A trivial lower bound on
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
is
\mathrm{IP}
, or equivalently
\mathrm{PSPACE}
, which can be easily seen by ignoring all but one provers. Extending the techniques in
[
6
]
to the quantum setting, Ito and Vidick proved the containment of
\mathrm{NEXP}
in
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}

[
42
]
. This lower bound was improved by a series of works in various settings
[
45
,
76
,
46
,
63
,
77
,
27
]
, which lead to a very recent breakthrough result
[
47
,
48
]
, in which Ji, Natarajan, Vidick, Wright and Yuen constructed a quantum multiprover interactive proof system for the Halting problem, and thus proved that
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}=\mathrm{RE}
, where
\mathrm{RE}
is the set of recursively enumerable languages.

This paper concerns
two-player one-round games
, a core model in computational complexity, which is closely related to multiprover interactive proof systems. A two-player one-round game
G
is run by three parties, a referee and two non-communicating players. We define
G=\left(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mu,V\right)
, where
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
are finite sets,
\mu
is a distribution over
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
and
V:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}\rightarrow\left\{0,1\right\}
is a predicate. Note that all
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mu,V
are public. The referee samples a pair of questions
\left(x,y\right)
according to
\mu
, and sends
x
and
y
to the two players separately. The two players have to provide an answer to the referee by choosing from
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, respectively, denoted by
\left(a,b\right)
. The referee accepts the answers he receives if and only if
V\left(x,y,a,b\right)=1
. The only restriction on the players’ strategies is that they are not allowed to exchange any information once the game has started. In the classical setting, the value of the game
\omega\left(G\right)
, the highest probability that the referee accepts the game, is

\omega\left(G\right)=\max_{h_{A}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\atop h_{B}:\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{B}}\sum_{xy}\mu\left(x,y\right)V(x,y,h_{A}(x),h_{B}(y)).

By the PCP theorem
[
2
,
3
]
it is
\mathrm{NP}
-hard to approximate
\omega(G)
within a multiplicative constant. The
entangled games
, which are the same as the classical games except that the players are allowed to share arbitrary entangled states before they receive the questions, were first introduced by Cleve, Høyer, Toner and Watrous
[
19
]
. They also defined the
entangled value
of a game, denoted by
\omega^{*}(G)
, to be the supremum of the probability that the referee accepts in a game when the players share entanglement,

\omega^{*}\left(G\right)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\max_{\psi_{AB}\in\mathcal{D}_{n}\atop\left\{P^{x}_{a}\right\}_{x,a},\left\{Q^{y}_{b}\right\}_{y,b}}\sum_{xy}\mu\left(x,y\right)\sum_{ab}V(x,y,a,b)\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P^{x}_{a}\otimes Q^{y}_{b}\right)\psi_{AB}\right),

(1)

where
\mathcal{D}_{n}
is the set of
n
-dimensional density operators,
\left\{P^{x}_{a}\right\}_{a}
and
\left\{Q^{y}_{b}\right\}_{b}
are POVMs for any
x\in\mathcal{X},y\in\mathcal{Y}
, respectively. Namely,
\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}P^{x}_{a}=\mathds{1}
,
\sum_{b\in\mathcal{B}}Q^{y}_{b}=\mathds{1}
,
P^{x}_{a}\geq 0
and
Q^{y}_{b}\geq 0
.

The quantities
\omega^{*}\left(G\right)
of nonlocal games are important in both physics and computer science. Cleve et al.
[
19
]
discovered the fact that
\omega^{*}(G)>\omega(G)
for the so-called
CHSH games

[
18
]
gives a re-interpretation of the
Bell’s inequalities

[
10
]
, which plays a central role in all aspects of quantum mechanics. It is nowadays known that there exist games for which
\omega^{*}(G)=1
while
\omega(G)
is arbitrarily small
[
70
,
1
]
. A large body of works have also been devoted to understanding the hardness of computing
\omega^{*}\left(G\right)
. It was shown in
[
53
,
41
]
that approximating
\omega^{*}(G)
to an inverse-polynomial accuracy is
\mathrm{NP}
-hard. Ji proved that it is
\mathrm{QMA}
-hard to approximate
\omega^{*}(G)
for multiplayer games to an inverse-exponential accuracy
[
45
]
. Later it was shown by the same author that approximating
\omega^{*}(G)
for multiplayer games to an inverse-polynomial accuracy is
\mathrm{MIP}^{*}
-complete
[
46
]
. Slofstra in his seminal results
[
76
,
77
]
proved that determining whether
\omega^{*}(G)=1
for general games
G
is undecidable. Moreover, a positive answer to the so-called Tsirelson’s problem (see e.g.
[
28
]
) implies the existence of an algorithm approximating
\omega^{*}(G)
for general games. It is known that Tsirelson’s problem is related to Connes’ Embedding Conjecture
[
21
]
, which was a longstanding open problem in functional analysis
[
50
,
67
]
. In
[
47
,
48
]
, Ji et al. completely resolved this problem by proving that even approximating
\omega^{*}(G)
for two-player nonlocal games to a constant accuracy is undecidable.

On the other hand, for a few known classes of games,
\omega^{*}(G)
is computable, and sometimes computing
\omega^{*}(G)
is even easier than computing
\omega(G)
. Cleve et al. in
[
19
]
gave a polynomial-time algorithm to exactly compute
\omega^{*}(G)
for XOR games
G
building on the work of Tsirelson
[
17
]
. Kempe, Regev and Toner later presented a polynomial-time algorithm for
\omega^{*}(G)
for unique games with a factor
6
approximation to
1-\omega^{*}(G)

[
52
]
. However, it is
\mathrm{NP}
-hard to approximate
\omega(G)
for XOR games within a factor of
11/12+\epsilon
and also
\mathrm{NP}
-hard to approximate
\omega(G)
for
k
-XOR games within a factor of
1/2+\epsilon
for
k\geq 3

[
38
]
. Classical unique games are conjectured to be NP-hard
[
54
]
as well.
Both of the algorithms in
[
19
,
52
]
are based on convex optimization. In particular, a hierarchy of semidefinite programs was proposed in
[
64
]
. The optimal values converge to
\omega^{*}_{\mathrm{co}}(G)
, which are the values of games when the players employ commutative strategies, and thus
\omega_{\mathrm{co}}\left(G\right)
is an upper bound on
\omega^{*}(G)
. However, the speed of convergence is unknown. There are some other classes of nonlocal games whose entangled values are known to be computable
[
85
]
. Readers may refer to the survey
[
81
]
for more details.

Main results

In this paper, we prove that for any nonlocal game, if the players share arbitrarily many copies of noisy maximally entangled states (MES) of a fixed dimension, then it is feasible to approximate the supremum of the winning probability to an arbitrary precision.

Theorem 1.1

(Main result, informal)
.

Given a nonlocal game
G
, for any integer
m\geq 2
, if the players share arbitrarily many copies of
m
-dimensional noisy MES
\Psi_{\epsilon}=\left(1-\epsilon\right)\left|\Psi_{m}\middle\rangle\middle\langle\Psi_{m}\right|+\epsilon\frac{\mathds{1}_{m}}{m}\otimes\frac{\mathds{1}_{m}}{m}
for any
\epsilon>0
, where
\left|\Psi_{m}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\left|i,i\right\rangle
is an
m
-dimensional MES, there exists an explicitly computable
D=D(\epsilon,\delta,m,G)
such that it suffices for the players to share
D
copies of
\psi
to achieve the winning probability at least
\omega^{*}\left(G,\Psi_{\epsilon}\right)-\delta
, where
\omega^{*}\left(G,\Psi_{\epsilon}\right)
represents the value of the game, which is the supremum of the winning probability that the players can achieve with arbitrarily many copies of preshared states
\Psi_{\epsilon}
. Thus, it is feasible to approximate the value of the game
\omega^{*}(G,\Psi_{\epsilon})
to arbitrary precision.

A natural and naive approach is to prove that the players are able to produce arbitrarily many copies of noisy MESs with bounded copies of perfect MESs and preshared classical randomness. The main result would be trivial if this were possible. However, this is not the case since the entanglement between the two players does not increase via local operations.

Ji et al. in
[
47
]
showed that if the players share arbitrarily many copies of perfect MES, then approximating the values of the games is as difficult as the Halting problem. Thus our result implies that the hardness of approximating nonlocal games is not robust against the noise of the preshared states.

The techniques developed in this paper are completely different from all previous approaches
[
19
,
52
,
64
,
8
,
28
,
76
,
85
,
77
,
47
]
. We generalize the framework of Boolean analysis, a well-studied and fruitful topic in theoretical computer science
[
66
]
, to matrix spaces and reduce the problem to
local state transformations

[
25
]
, a quantum analog of
non-interactive simulations of joint distributions
. This approach provides a new perspective with novel tools to study entangled nonlocal games. We develop a series of results about Fourier analysis on matrix spaces, which might be of independent of interest and have applications in quantum property testing, quantum machine learning, etc.

Non-interactive simulations of joint distributions are a fundamental problem in information theory and communication complexity. Consider two non-communicating players Alice and Bob, and suppose they are provided a sequence of independent samples
\left(x_{1},y_{1}\right),\left(x_{2},y_{2}\right),\ldots
from a joint distribution
\mu
on
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, where Alice observes
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
and Bob observes
y_{1},y_{2},\ldots
. Without communicating with each other, for what joint distribution
\nu
can Alice and Bob sample? The research on this problem dates back to the classic works by Gács and Körner
[
29
]
, Wyner
[
88
]
and Witsenhausen
[
86
]
, followed by fruitful subsequent works (see, for example,
[
51
]
and the references therein). Recently, Ghazi, Kamath and Sudan in
[
32
]
studied the decidability of non-interactive simulations of joint distributions by introducing a framework built on the theory of Boolean analysis and Hermitian analysis
[
62
,
61
,
66
]
. Using this framework, the decidability is resolved in subsequent works
[
24
,
31
]
.

In the quantum universe, it is natural to consider the quantum analog of non-interactive simulations of joint distributions, which is also referred to as local state transformations. Suppose the two non-communicating players Alice and Bob are provided with arbitrarily many copies of bipartite quantum states
\psi_{AB}
. Without communicating with each other, what bipartite quantum state
\phi_{AB}
can Alice and Bob jointly create? Delgosha and Beigi first studied this problem and gave a necessary condition for the exact local state transformation of
\psi_{AB}
to
\phi_{AB}

[
25
]
. Other than this result, not much about this problem is known. Our paper essentially resolves the decidability of local state transformations, when
\psi_{AB}
is a noisy MES and the target state
\phi_{AB}
is a classical bipartite distribution. The proofs adopt the framework laid down in
[
32
]
and its subsequent works
[
24
,
31
]
. It heavily uses Boolean analysis and Hermitian analysis on Gaussian spaces, which have been intensively studied and have rich applications in theoretical computer science
[
66
]
. Some key components in Boolean analysis and Hermitian analysis, such as
hypercontractive inequalities
, have also been extended to quantum settings in various aspects, which have led to several interesting applications
[
11
,
60
,
79
,
56
,
25
,
22
]
.
However, much less is known about Fourier analysis on matrix spaces or quantum operations compared to Boolean analysis or Hermitian analysis on Gaussian spaces. We systematically develop Fourier analysis on
random matrix spaces
, which are hybrids of matrix spaces and Gaussian spaces. In particular, this paper proves a
quantum invariance principle
and a hypercontractive inequality for
random operators
, successfully generalizing the framework established in
[
32
,
24
,
31
]
to the quantum setting. Invariance principles are a core result in the analysis of Boolean functions, which have a number of applications in various areas(see
[
37
]
and the references therein). To the best of our knowledge, this is the first quantum invariance principle and the result in this paper is the first application of the invariance principle in quantum complexity theory. We believe that the tools developed in this paper are interesting in their own right and have further applications.

1.1
Technical Contributions

In this paper, we treat the set
\mathcal{M}_{m}
of
m\times m
matrices as a Hilbert space of dimension
m^{2}
by introducing the inner product
\left\langle A,B\right\rangle=\frac{1}{m}\mathrm{Tr}A^{\dagger}B
. Let
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\mathcal{B}_{0},\mathcal{B}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m^{2}-1}\right\}
be an orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
with all elements being Hermitian and
\mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathds{1}
, whose existence is guaranteed by
Lemma

2.10
. It is easy to verify that the set
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}:\sigma\in\left\{0,\ldots,m^{2}-1\right\}^{n}\right\}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{def}}}{{=}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{n}}
, forms an orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
. Any operator
P\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
can be expressed as

P=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

(2)

which can be viewed as a Fourier expansion of
P
in terms of the basis
\mathcal{B}
, where
[n]_{\geq 0}
represents the set
\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}
.

Quantum invariance principle and quantum hypercontractive inequality

Invariance principles are a powerful tool in Boolean analysis, which has found applications in various areas in theoretical computer science, such as inapproximation theory, derandomization, voting theory, etc
[
62
]
. Let’s recall the invariance principle for functions in
[
62
]
. Let
f:\left\{1,-1\right\}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
be a bounded-degree multilinear polynomial with small influence for all variables. An invariance principle asserts that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\sim\left\{1,-1\right\}^{n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\Psi\left(f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right)\right]\approx\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}\sim\gamma_{n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\Psi\left(f\left(\mathbf{g}\right)\right)\right]

(3)

for any Lipschitz continuous function
\Psi:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, where
\gamma_{n}
is a standard
n
-dimensional normal distribution. Here the expression "
f\left(\mathbf{g}\right)
" is an abuse of notation indicating that the real numbers
\mathbf{g}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{n}
are substituted into
f
’s Fourier expansion. The power of Eq.
Eq.

3
is that we are able to interchange arbitrary random variables with Gaussian random variables.

One of our main contributions is establishing a quantum invariance principle. Let
P\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
be a Hermitian operator with a Fourier expansion in Eq.
Eq.

2
satisfying that all registers have low influence (which will be specified). Here we view
P
as an operator acting on
n
-partite quantum systems, where each system is of dimension
m
. The term "register" is referred to each system. For a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
piecewise polynomial
\Psi
, it holds that

\frac{1}{m^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}~\Psi\left(\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\right)\approx\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{g}\sim\gamma_{\left(m^{2}-1\right)n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\Psi\left(\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{g}_{i,\sigma_{i}}\right)\right],

(4)

where
\mathbf{g}=\left(\mathbf{g}_{1,1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{{}_{1},m^{2}-1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{n,1},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{{}_{1},m^{2}-1}\right)\sim\gamma_{n\left(m^{2}-1\right)}
and
\mathbf{g}_{1,0}=\cdots=\mathbf{g}_{n,0}=1
.
Eq.
Eq.

4
enables us to turn the quantum registers to Gaussian random variables, which gives polynomials in Gaussian spaces.

The proof of Eq.
Eq.

3
employs the well-known Lindeberg-style method (see e.g., Chapter 11 in
[
66
]
). It first substitutes
\Psi
by a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
approximation
\Psi^{\prime}
, whose existence follows by standard results in approximation theory. Then by expanding both sides of Eq.
Eq.

3
to the third-order derivatives via Taylor series, we can prove that the difference between the left-hand side and the right-hand side is upper bounded by the norms of the third-order terms in the Taylor expansion of
\Psi^{\prime}
. Applying the hypercontractive inequality for Boolean variables and the one for Gaussian variables, respectively, we can see that the difference between the two sides is small for functions with all variables having low influence.

Generalizing the Lindeberg-style method to matrices is not an easy task due to the non-commutativity of matrices. To this end, we need to investigate the analytical properties of matrix-functions. We use Fréchet derivatives, a notion of derivatives in Banach spaces, in which a similar form of Taylor expansions exists. The differentiability of real functions and of matrix-functions with respect to Fréchet derivatives share many properties in common
[
73
]
. We follow the same mechanism as the original Lindeberg-style method by substituting the basis elements with Gaussian variables and obtain random operators, which are hybrids of operators and random variables. The Taylor expansions of matrix-valued functions are in general complicated due to the nature of the non-commutativity as well. Fortunately, it suffices to prove a quantum invariance principle for a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
piecewise polynomial for our purpose.

We prove that the difference between the two sides in Eq.
Eq.

4
is upper bounded by the 3rd order term in the Taylor expansion of
\Psi
, which, in turn, is upper bounded by the product of its
2
-norm and the square of its
4
-norm. Applying the hypercontractive inequality for random operators, we are able to upper bound the difference by the cube of its
2
-norm, which is further upper bounded by the influences of all registers.

Another difficulty that follows is to prove a hypercontractive inequality for random operators, which is expected to show that the
4
-norm of a bounded-degree random operator can be upper bounded by its
2
-norm. As random operators are hybrids of operators and Gaussian variables, our proof delicately combines the hypercontractivity for Gaussian variables and the hypercontractivity for operators. The former has already been proved in
[
87
]
. King in
[
55
]
proved a hypercontractive inequality for unital qubit channels, which immediately implies a hypercontractive inequality for operators in
\mathcal{M}_{2}^{\otimes n}
. However, since his proof heavily relies on the properties of qubit channels, it fails to generalize to
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
for
m>2
. This paper focuses only on the hypercontractivity for depolarizing channels in any dimension. Thus we use the properties specific to depolarizing channels to prove a hypercontractive inequality for operators via an inductive argument. The desired result follows after we combine a hypercontractive inequality for operators and a hypercontractive inequality for random variables in
[
87
]
.

Quantum dimension reductions

Dimension reductions are a versatile tool in theoretical computer science. One of the most well-known dimension reduction techniques is arguably the Johnson–Lindenstrauss lemma
[
49
]
, which enables us to embed a set of points from a high-dimensional space into a space of much lower dimension with slight distortion. Dimension reductions are a natural approach to upper bound the complexity of quantum nonlocal games. However, proving quantum dimension reductions seems to be difficult and sometimes even impossible
[
36
,
78
]
. In this paper, we present a new quantum dimension reduction technique by establishing a new quantum invariance principle and combining it with a recent dimension reduction result in Gaussian spaces
[
31
]
.

To the best of our knowledge, this is the first invariance principle in the quantum setting. We are also not aware of any other invariance principle proved via Fréchet derivatives prior to our result. We believe our invariance principle and quantum dimension reduction are interesting in their own right and should have further applications.

1.2
Proof Overview

We are interested in the decidability of the following decision problem, which is a special case of local state transformations.

Q
. Given
0<\epsilon,\delta<1
, integers
t,m\geq 2
and a distribution
\mu
over
[t]\times[t]
, suppose Alice and Bob share arbitrarily many copies of the bipartite quantum states
\Psi_{\epsilon}=\left(1-\epsilon\right)\left|\Psi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\Psi\right|+\epsilon\frac{\mathds{1}_{m}}{m}\otimes\frac{\mathds{1}_{m}}{m}
, where
\left|\Psi\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\left|i,i\right\rangle
is an
m
-dimensional maximally entangled state.

Yes
. Alice and Bob are able to jointly sample a distribution
\mu^{\prime}
over
[t]\times[t]
which is
\delta
-close to
\mu
, i.e.,
\left\|\mu^{\prime}-\mu\right\|_{1}\leq\delta
.

No
. Any distribution
\mu^{\prime}
over
[t]\times[t]
that Alice and Bob can jointly sample is
2\delta
-far from
\mu
, i.e.,
\left\|\mu-\mu^{\prime}\right\|_{1}\geq 2\delta
.

Special case:
\mu
is a binary distribution

We first consider the special case that
\mu
is a binary distribution, namely
t=2
. Suppose the POVM’s performed by Alice and Bob are
\left\{P,\mathds{1}-P\right\}
and
\left\{Q,\mathds{1}-Q\right\}
, respectively, where
P,Q\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
and
0\leq P,Q\leq\mathds{1}
. Here
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
represents the set of all Hermitian operators acting on an
n
-qudit quantum system, where each qudit is of dimension
m
. The proof is to construct a universal bound
D
, which is independent of the measurements, and transformations
f_{n},g_{n}:\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes D}
, such that the requirements in
Fig.

1
are satisfied.

Requirements.

1.

0\leq f_{n}\left(P\right)\leq\mathds{1}~\mbox{and}~0\leq g_{n}\left(Q\right)\leq\mathds{1};

2.

\frac{1}{m^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}~P\approx\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~f_{n}\left(P\right)~\mbox{and}~\frac{1}{m^{n}}\mathrm{Tr}~Q_{n}\approx\frac{1}{m^{D}}\mathrm{Tr}~g_{n}\left(Q\right);

3.

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P\otimes Q\right)\Psi_{\epsilon}^{\otimes n}\right)\approx\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(f_{n}\left(P\right)\otimes g_{n}\left(Q\right)\right)\Psi_{\epsilon}^{\otimes D}\right).

Figure 1:
Requirements for binary output distributions

The first item implies that
\left\{f_{n}\left(P\right),\mathds{1}-f_{n}\left(P\right)\right\}
and
\left\{g_{n}\left(Q\right),\mathds{1}-g_{n}\left(Q\right)\right\}
are both valid POVMs. The second item implies that the probability that Alice outputs
0
is almost unchanged under the transformation
f_{n}
. Same for the probability that Bob outputs
0
. The last item implies that the probability that both Alice and Bob output
0
is almost unchanged. As Alice’s and Bob’s outputs are both binary, it follows that the distribution of the joint output is almost unchanged.

The constructions of the transformations
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
are built on the framework introduced in
[
32
]
, which, in turn, was based on the results in Boolean analysis and Hermitian analysis
[
62
,
61
]
. Let
\mathcal{A}=\left\{\mathcal{A}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{m^{2}-1}\right\}
and
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\mathcal{B}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m^{2}-1}\right\}
, which will be specified later, be two properly chosen orthonormal bases in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
. The Fourier expansions of
P
and
Q
on bases
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
can be expressed as

P=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}~\mbox{and}~Q=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{Q}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

respectively, where
[n]_{\geq 0}
represents the set
\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}
. The steps for constructing the transformations
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
are specified as follows.

•

Smooth operators
.

Objective
:

–

bounded-degree approximation.

We first convert the operators
\left(P,Q\right)
to a new pair of operators that can be approximated by bounded-degree operators via smoothing operations. The standard techniques in Boolean analysis
[
66
]
motivate us to apply a
noise operator

\Delta_{\rho}
for some
\rho\in(0,1)
to both
P
and
Q
. Note that the noise operator is also referred to as a depolarizing channel in quantum information theory
[
65
]
, and its counterpart in the Gaussian space is called
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator
.
Then we obtain

\Delta_{\rho}P=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\rho^{|\sigma|}\mathcal{A}_{\sigma}~\mbox{and}~\Delta_{\rho}Q=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{Q}\left(\sigma\right)\rho^{|\sigma|}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

where
|\sigma|=\left|\left\{i:\sigma_{i}\neq 0\right\}\right|
.
Here
\Delta_{\rho}P
and
\Delta_{\rho}Q
are independent of the choices of bases by
Definition

3.4
and
Lemma

3.5
item 1. It is not hard to verify that the requirements specified in item 1 and item 2 in
Fig.

1
are satisfied. To meet the requirements in item 3, we need the notion of
quantum maximal correlation
introduced by Beigi
[
9
]
, which extends the
maximal correlation coefficients

[
39
,
30
,
71
]
in classical information theory. Note that after smoothing the operators, the weights of high-degree parts of both
P
and
Q
, namely, the parts with high
\left|\sigma\right|
, decrease exponentially. Thus, both operators can be approximated by bounded-degree operators.

•

Joint regularity
.

Objective
:

–

bounded-degree approximation;

–

bounded number of high-influential registers.

We prove that the number of high-influential registers is bounded. Let
H
be the set of all high-influential registers. The influence of a register to a Hermitian operator on a multipartite quantum system is defined in
Definition

2.2
, which was first introduced by Montanaro in
[
60
]
, analogous to the
influence
defined in Boolean analysis
[
66
]
. It informally speaking, measures how much the register can influence the operator. This step follows by quantizing a well-known result in Boolean analysis. For any bounded function, the total influence, i.e., the summation of the influences of all variables, is upper bounded by the degree of the function. Note that both
P
and
Q
can be approximated by bounded-degree operators after the first step. By Markov’s inequality, the size of
H
can be bounded.

•

Invariance from operators to random operators
.

Objective
:

–

bounded-degree approximation;

–

bounded number of quantum registers;

–

unbounded number of Gaussian variables.

In this step, we use correlated Gaussian variables to substitute for all the quantum registers in
P
and
Q
with low influence, after which we get random operators.

Prior to the substitution, we prove that there exist bases
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
such that

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathcal{A}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{j}\right)\psi_{AB}\right)=\delta_{i,j}c_{i},

for
1=c_{0}\geq c_{1}\geq c_{2}\geq\cdots\geq c_{m^{2}-1}\geq 0
, where
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathds{1}
.
Hence

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P\otimes Q\right)\psi_{AB}^{\otimes n}\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}c_{\sigma}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\widehat{Q}\left(\sigma\right),

(5)

where
c_{\sigma}=c_{\sigma_{1}}\cdot c_{\sigma_{2}}\cdots c_{\sigma_{n}}
for any
\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}
.

We now introduce independent joint random variables

\left\{\left(\mathbf{g}_{i,j},\mathbf{h}_{i,j}\right)\right\}_{i\notin H,j\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}},

where
\mathbf{g}_{i,0}=\mathbf{h}_{i,0}=1
and
\left(\mathbf{g}_{i,j},\mathbf{h}_{i,j}\right)\sim\mathcal{G}_{c_{j}}
for
i\notin H,j\geq 1
and
\rho
-correlated two-dimensional Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{G}_{\rho}
. Given the Fourier expansions of
P
and
Q
, we substitute all the matrix bases in the quantum registers not in
H
by
\left(\mathbf{g}_{i,j},\mathbf{h}_{i,j}\right)
, respectively, to obtain the random operators distributed over all operators acting on the registers in
H
as follows.

\mathbf{P}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\left(\prod_{i\notin H}\mathbf{g}_{i,\sigma_{i}}\right)\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{H}}

(6)

and

\mathbf{Q}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{Q}\left(\sigma\right)\left(\prod_{i\notin H}\mathbf{h}_{i,\sigma_{i}}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}}.

It is easy to verify the requirements specified by item 2 and item 3 in
Fig.

1
are satisfied in expectation. However,
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
are in general not POVM elements, which means that item 1 is violated. To meet item 1, it suffices to show that both of the random operators are
\ell_{2}
-close to POVM elements in expectation. Let
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
be a rounding map of the set of POVM elements, namely
\mathcal{R}\left(X\right)=\arg\min\left\{\left\|X-Y\right\|_{2}:0\leq Y\leq\mathds{1}\right\}
, where
\left\|\cdot\right\|_{2}
is the Schatten 2-norm. Hence, we prove that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left\|\mathbf{P}-\mathcal{R}\left(\mathbf{P}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}\right]\approx 0~\mbox{and}~\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left\|\mathbf{Q}-\mathcal{R}\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)\right\|^{2}_{2}\right]\approx 0.

(7)

It is not hard to verify that

\left\|\mathcal{R}(X)-X\right\|_{2}^{2}=\mathrm{Tr}~\theta\left(X\right)
, where

\theta\left(x\right)=\begin{cases}(x-1)^{2}~&\mbox{if $x\geq 1$}\\
x^{2}~&\mbox{if $x\leq 0$}\\
0~&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(8)

Thus, proving
Eq.

7
is equivalent to proving that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\theta\left(\mathbf{P}\right)\right]\approx 0~\mbox{and}~\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Tr}~\theta\left(\mathbf{Q}\right)\right]\approx 0.

(9)

As described above, we need to prove an invariance principle for the function
\theta
defined in Eq.
Eq.

9
, which is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
piecewise polynomial. We are able to construct a
\mathcal{C}^{2}
approximation of
\theta
, denoted by
\theta_{\lambda}
, which is also a piecewise polynomial. We prove an invariance principle for
\theta_{\lambda}\left(\cdot\right)
and show that both
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{i}\mathrm{Tr}~\theta\left(\mathbf{P}_{i}\right)\right]
and
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{i}\mathrm{Tr}~\theta\left(\mathbf{Q}_{i}\right)\right]
are upper bounded by the 3rd order terms in the Taylor expansion of
\theta_{\lambda}
, which, in turn, are upper bounded by the cube of their
2
-norm due to a hypercontractive inequality for random operators.

•

Dimension reduction
.

Objective
:

–

bounded number of quantum registers;

–

bounded number of Gaussian variables.

For any
\sigma_{H}\in[m^{2}]^{|H|}_{\geq 0}
, let

p_{\sigma_{H}}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{g}}}\right)=\sum_{\tau\in[m^{2}]^{n}_{\geq 0}:\tau_{H}=\sigma_{H}}\widehat{P}\left(\tau\right)\left(\prod_{i\notin H}\mathbf{g}_{i,\tau_{i}}\right)

and

q_{\sigma_{H}}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{h}}}\right)=\sum_{\tau\in[m^{2}]^{n}_{\geq 0}:\tau_{H}=\sigma_{H}}\widehat{Q}\left(\tau\right)\left(\prod_{i\notin H}\mathbf{g}_{i,\tau_{i}}\right),

where
p_{\sigma_{H}},q_{\sigma_{H}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\left(m^{2}-1\right)(n-|H|)}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{g}}}=\left(\mathbf{g}_{i,j}\right)_{i\notin H,j\in[m^{2}-1]},\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{h}}}=\left(\mathbf{h}_{i,j}\right)_{i\notin H,j\in[m^{2}-1]}.

Then

\left(p_{\sigma_{H}}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{g}}}\right)\right)_{\sigma_{H}\in[m^{2}]^{|H|}_{\geq 0}}
and
\left(q_{\sigma_{H}}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{h}}}\right)\right)_{\sigma_{H}\in[m^{2}]^{|H|}_{\geq 0}}
are the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
, respectively. Namely,

\mathbf{P}=\sum_{\sigma_{H}\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{|H|}}p_{\sigma_{H}}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{g}}}\right)\mathcal{A}_{\sigma_{H}}~\mbox{and}~\mathbf{Q}=\sum_{\sigma_{H}\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{|H|}}q_{\sigma_{H}}\left(\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{\mathbf{h}}}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{H}},

(10)

After applying the
dimension reduction
in
[
31
]
to polynomials
\left(p_{\sigma_{H}},q_{\sigma_{H}}\right)
, the number of Gaussian random variables is reduced to a bounded number. Meanwhile, all the requirements listed in
Fig.

1
are still satisfied in expectation.

•

Smooth random operators
.

Objective
:

–

bounded-degree approximation;

–

bounded number of quantum registers;

–

bounded number of Gaussian variables.

The remaining steps are mainly concerned with removing the Gaussian variables in
\mathbf{P}
and
\mathbf{Q}
to obtain POVM elements. To this end, we perform transformations similar to the ones in the previous steps. We first apply the noise operator again to both operators to reduce the weight of the high degree parts.

•

Multilinearization
.

Objective
:

–

bounded-degree approximation;

–

bounded number of quantum registers;

–

bounded number of Gaussian variables;

–

multilinear.

The degrees of Gaussian variables occurring in the functions might be unbounded. Prior to the substitution of Gaussian variables by quantum registers, we need the multilinearization lemma in
[
31
]
to reduce the power of each Gaussian variable to either
0
or
1
. Namely, the polynomials
p_{\sigma_{H}}
’s and
q_{\sigma_{H}}
’s in Eq.
Eq.

10
are all multilinear after this step.

•

Invariance from random operators to operators
.

Objective
:

–

bounded number of quantum registers.

In the final step, we substitute the Gaussian variables by properly chosen basis elements and round both operators to POVM elements. Again, we need to apply a quantum invariance principle to ensure that all the requirements in
Fig.

1
are satisfied.

General case:
\mu
is a general bipartite distribution

Suppose Alice and Bob share
n
copies of
\Psi_{\epsilon}
and the POVMs Alice and Bob peform are
\left(P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}\right)
and
\left(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{t}\right)
, respectively.
We need to construct a universal bound
D
, which is independent of the measurements, and the transformations
f_{n},g_{n}:\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes D}
such that the requirements in
Fig.

2
are satisfied. Note that item 1 (b) requires that the output of the transformations is a pair of sub-POVMs rather than POVMs. However, it can be proved that this technical constraint does not affect our final conclusion.

Requirements

Let
\left(\widetilde{P}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{P}_{t}\right)=f_{n}\left(P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}\right)
and
\left(\widetilde{Q}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{t}\right)=g_{n}\left(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{t}\right)
.

1.

\left(\widetilde{P}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{P}_{t}\right)
and
\left(\widetilde{Q}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{Q}_{t}\right)
are valid sub-POVMs. Namely,

(a)

\forall i,j\in[t]~0\leq\widetilde{P}_{i}\leq\mathds{1}
and
0\leq\widetilde{Q}_{i}\leq\mathds{1}
.

(b)

\sum_{i=1}^{t}\widetilde{P}_{i}\leq\mathds{1}
and
\sum_{i=1}^{t}\widetilde{Q}_{i}\leq\mathds{1}
.

2.

\forall i,j\in[t]~\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P_{i}\otimes Q_{j}\right)\Psi_{m}^{\otimes n}\right)\approx\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\widetilde{P}_{i}\otimes\widetilde{Q}_{j}\right)\Psi_{m}^{\otimes D}\right).

Figure 2:
Requirements for general output distributions

A natural approach is to apply the transformations
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
constructed for the binary case to all
P_{i}
’s and
Q_{i}
’s, respectively. Note that Eq.
Eq.

9
and the quantum invariance principle guarantee item 1(a). However, as the transformations are applied to
P_{i}
’s and
Q_{i}
’s individually, we cannot directly conclude item 1(b). The difficulty arises when trying to prove item 1(b) in
Fig.

2
as it enforces global constraints on
P_{i}
’s and
Q_{i}
’s.

Given
X_{1},\dots,X_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
, let

\mathcal{R}\left(X_{1},\ldots,X_{t}\right)=\arg\min\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{t}\left\|X_{i}-Y_{i}\right\|_{2}^{2}:\left(\forall i\right)0\leq Y_{i}\leq\mathds{1},\sum_{i}Y_{i}\leq\mathds{1}\right\}

be a rounding map of the set of all sub-POVMs. And

\theta\left(X_{1},\ldots,X_{t}\right)=\left\|\left(X_{1},\ldots,X_{t}\right)-\mathcal{R}\left(X_{1},\ldots,X_{t}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(11)

To obtain a similar result as Eq.
Eq.

9
, an intuitive approach is to prove a quantum version of the multivariable invariance principle
[
40
]
. However, this is technically challenging. In the classical setting, i.e., all
X_{i}
’s are real numbers, to establish a multivariable invariance principle, it requires us to compute the Taylor expansion of a smooth approximating function for
\theta

[
40
]
. However, the high-order Fréchet derivatives of the functions with multiple matrices as inputs are highly involved in general.

To avoid the difficulty of analyzing functions with multiple matrices as inputs, we upper bound the function
\theta
in Eq.
Eq.

11
by a function that takes only a single matrix as input. Namely,

\theta\left(P_{1},\ldots,P_{t}\right)\leq O\left(t\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{t}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(P_{i}\right)\right),

(12)

where

\zeta\left(x\right)=\begin{cases}x^{2}~&\mbox{if $x\leq 0$}\\
0~&\mbox{otherwise}\end{cases}.

(13)

With Eq.
Eq.

12
, it is sufficient for us to prove that

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{i}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(\mathbf{P}_{i}\right)\right]\approx 0~\mbox{and}~\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\sum_{i}\mathrm{Tr}~\zeta\left(\mathbf{Q}_{i}\right)\right]\approx 0,

(14)

which is again obtained by a quantum invariance principle described in the previous subsection.

The constructions of the maps
f_{n}
and
g_{n}
are summarized in
Fig.

3
.

0\leq P,Q\leq\mathds{1}

Figure 3:
Construction of the transformations

Note that in nonlocal games the players perform different measurements for different inputs. Thus, we may encounter consistency issues when applying the transformations mentioned above to nonlocal games. Suppose the players’ strategies are
\left(\left\{P,\mathds{1}-P\right\},\left\{Q,\mathds{1}-Q\right\}\right)
and
\left(\left\{P,\mathds{1}-P\right\},\left\{Q^{\prime},\mathds{1}-Q^{\prime}\right\}\right)
for questions
(x,y)
and
\left(x,y^{\prime}\right)
, respectively. We need to ensure that the resulting POVMs on Alice’s side are same in both cases. Namely, the transformation for each player should be independent of the other player. Note that only the regularization and dimension reduction steps jointly depend on both operators. Fortunately, the dependence between the two players in both steps can be removed by applying a union bound on all of the possible question pairs.

Organization

Section

2
summarizes some useful concepts and basic facts on quantum mechanics, the analysis on Gaussian spaces, matrix spaces and random operators.
Section

3
introduces the notion of quantum maximal correlations, a key concept in this work, with several crucial properties. The main results and the proofs are stated and proved in
Section

4
. A summary with further work and open problems are listed in
Section

5
. The step of smoothing operators is in
Section

6
.
Section

7
proves a joint regularity lemma.
Section

8
proves a hypercontractive inequality for random operators.
Section

9
presents a reduction from the rounding distance to the function
\mathrm{Tr}\zeta\left(\cdot\right)
. Quantum invariance principles are proved in
Section

10
.
Section

11
proves a dimension reduction for random operators. The step of smoothing random operators is presented in
Section

12
. The step of multilinearization is proved in
Section

13
.
Appendix

A
summarizes basic facts on Fréchet derivatives.
Appendix

B
and
Appendix

C
present some deferred proofs.
Appendix

D
summarizes all the notations in a table for readers to look up.

2
Preliminaries

This paper uses a number of notations. For readers’ convenience, all the notations are summarized in
Appendix

D
. For an integer
n\geq 1
, let
[n]
and
[n]_{\geq 0}
represent the sets
\left\{1,\ldots,n\right\}
and
\left\{0,\ldots,n-1\right\}
, respectively. Given a finite set
\mathcal{X}
and a natural number
k
, let
\mathcal{X}^{k}
be the set
\mathcal{X}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{X}
, the Cartesian product of
\mathcal{X}
,
k
times. Given
a=a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
and a set
S\subseteq[k]
, we write
a_{S}
to represent the projection of
a
to the coordinates specified in
S
. For any
i\in[k]
,
a_{-i}
represents
a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1},a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{n}
and
a_{<i}
represents
a_{1},\ldots,a_{i-1}
.
a_{\leq i},a_{>i},a_{\geq i}
are defined similarly. For any
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{k}
, we define
\left|\sigma\right|=\left|\left\{i:\sigma_{i}\neq 0\right\}\right|
and
\mathrm{wt}\left(\sigma\right)=\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}
. Let
\mu
be a probability distribution on
\mathcal{X}
, and
\mu\left(x\right)
represent the probability of
x\in\mathcal{X}
according to
\mu
. Let
X
be a random variable distributed according to
\mu
. We use the same symbol to represent a random variable and its distribution whenever it is clear from the context. The expectation of a function
f
on
\mathcal{X}
is defined as

\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[f(X)\right]=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\sim X\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[f(\mathbf{x})\right]=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\Pr\>\!\!\left[X=x\right]\cdot f\left(x\right)=\sum_{x}\mu\left(x\right)\cdot f\left(x\right),

where
\mathbf{x}\sim X
represents that
\mathbf{x}
is drawn according to
X
. For any two distributions
\mu
and
\nu
,
\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\right)\sim\mu\otimes\nu
represents that
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
are drawn from
\mu
and
\nu
, independently. For two distributions
p
and
q
, the
\ell_{1}
-distance between
p
and
q
is defined to be

\left\|p-q\right\|_{1}=\sum_{x}\left|p\left(x\right)-q\left(x\right)\right|.

In this paper, the lower-cased letters in bold
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\cdots
are reserved for random variables. The capital letters in bold,
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q},\ldots
are reserved for random operators defined below.

2.1
Quantum mechanics

We briefly review the formalism of quantum mechanics over a finite dimensional system. For a more thorough treatment, readers may refer to
[
65
,
84
]
. Given a quantum system
A
, it is associated with a finite dimensional Hilbert space, which, by abuse of notation, is also denoted by
A
. We denote by
\mathcal{M}\left(A\right)
and
\mathcal{H}\left(A\right)
the set of all linear operators and the set of all Hermitian operators in the space, respectively. The identity operator in
A
is denoted by
\mathds{1}_{A}
. If the dimension of
A
is
m
, then we write
\mathcal{M}\left(A\right)=\mathcal{M}_{m}
,
\mathcal{H}\left(A\right)=\mathcal{H}_{m}
and
\mathds{1}_{A}=\mathds{1}_{m}
. The subscripts may be dropped whenever it is clear from the context. A quantum state in the quantum system
A
is represented by a
density operator

\rho_{A}
, a positive semidefinite operator over the Hilbert space
A
with unit trace. We denote by
\mathcal{D}\left(A\right)
the set of all density operators in
A
. A quantum state is
pure
if the density operator is a rank-one projector
\left|\psi\middle\rangle\middle\langle\psi\right|
, which is also represented by
\left|\psi\right\rangle
for convenience. Composite quantum systems are associated with the
(Kronecker) tensor product space
of the underlying spaces, i.e., for the quantum systems
A
and
B
, the composition of the two systems are represented by
A\otimes B
with the sets of the linear operators, the Hermitian operators and the density operators denoted by
\mathcal{M}\left(A\otimes B\right)
,
\mathcal{H}\left(A\otimes B\right)
and
\mathcal{D}\left(A\otimes B\right)
, respectively. We sometimes use the shorthand
AB
for
A\otimes B
. The sets of the linear operators and the Hermitian operators in the composition of
n

m
-dimensional Hilbert spaces are denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
and
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}
, respectively. With a slight abuse of notations, we assume that
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}={\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n}={\mathbb{R}}
when
n=0
. A
quantum channel
from the input system
A
to the output system
B
is represented by a
completely positive, trace-preserving linear map
(CPTP map). The set of all quantum channels from a system
A
to a system
B
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}\left(A,B\right)
. A quantum channel in
\mathcal{L}\left(A,B\right)
is
unital
if it maps
\mathds{1}_{A}
to
\mathds{1}_{B}
. A
quantum operator
on
A
is a channel with both the input system and the output system being
A
. The set of the quantum operators on
A
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}\left(A\right)
. An important operation on a composite system
A\otimes B
is the
partial trace

\mathrm{Tr}_{B}\left(\cdot\right)
which effectively derives the marginal state of the subsystem
A
from the quantum state
\rho_{AB}
. The partial trace is given by

\mathrm{Tr}_{B}\rho_{AB}=\sum_{i}\left(\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\left\langle i\right|\right)\rho_{AB}\left(\mathds{1}_{A}\otimes\left|i\right\rangle\right)

where
\left\{\left|i\right\rangle\right\}
is an orthonormal basis in
B
. The partial trace is a valid quantum channel in
\mathcal{L}\left(A\otimes B,A\right)
. Note that the action is independent of the choices of basis
\left\{\left|i\right\rangle\right\}
, so we unambiguously write
\rho_{A}=\mathrm{Tr}_{B}\rho_{AB}
. In this paper, we may also apply the partial trace to an arbitrary operator in
\mathcal{M}\left(A\otimes B\right)
. A pure state evolution on a system
A
with a state
\left|\psi\right\rangle
is represented by a unitary operator
U^{A}
, denoted by
U^{A}\left|\psi\right\rangle
. An evolution on the register
B
of a state
\left|\psi\right\rangle_{AB}
under the action of a unitary
U^{B}
is represented by
\left(\mathds{1}^{A}\otimes U^{B}\right)\left|\psi\right\rangle_{AB}
. The superscripts and the subscripts might be dropped whenever it is clear from the context. A
quantum measurement
is represented by a
positive-operator valued measure
(POVM), which is a set of positive semidefinite operators
\left\{M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\right\}
satisfying
\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}=\mathds{1}
, where
n
is the number of possible measurement outcomes. Suppose the state of the quantum system is
\rho
. The probability that it outputs
i
is
\mathrm{Tr}M_{i}\rho
. In this paper, we use
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{M}}=\left(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\right)
to represent an ordered set of operators. We say
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{M}}=\left(M_{1},\ldots,M_{n}\right)
is a POVM if for each i
M_{i}\geq 0
is a POVM element with the corresponding output
i
and
\sum_{i}M_{i}=\mathds{1}
. We say
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\longrightarrow}}{{M}}
is a
sub-POVM
if
M_{i}\geq 0
for
1\leq i\leq n
and
\sum_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}\leq\mathds{1}
.

In this paper, we need the following fact.

Fact 2.1
.

Given registers
A,B
, operators
P\in\mathcal{H}\left(A\right),Q\in\mathcal{H}\left(B\right)
and a bipartite state
\psi_{AB}
, it holds that

1.

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P\otimes\mathds{1}_{B}\right)\psi_{AB}\right)=\mathrm{Tr}P\psi_{A}
;

2.

\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P\otimes Q\right)\psi_{AB}\right)\right|\leq\left(\mathrm{Tr}P^{2}\psi_{A}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\left(\mathrm{Tr}Q^{2}\psi_{B}\right)^{1/2}
.

Proof.

Item 1 follows from definitions.

For item 2, by Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(P\otimes Q\right)\psi_{AB}\right)\right|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{Tr}\left(\sqrt{\psi_{AB}}\left(P\otimes\mathds{1}\right)\left(\mathds{1}\otimes Q\right)\sqrt{\psi_{AB}}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Tr}\left(P\otimes\mathds{1}\right)^{2}\psi_{AB}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\left(\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathds{1}\otimes Q\right)^{2}\psi_{AB}\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Tr}P^{2}\psi_{A}\right)^{1/2}\cdot\left(\mathrm{Tr}Q^{2}\psi_{B}\right)^{1/2}\quad\quad\mbox{(item 1)}

∎

2.2
Gaussian spaces

For
p\geq 1
and any integer
n>0
, let
\gamma_{n}
represent a standard
n
-dimensional normal distribution. A function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
is in
L^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
if

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\left|f(x)\right|^{p}\gamma_{n}\left(dx\right)<\infty.

All the functions considered in this paper are in
L^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
for all
p\geq 1
unless otherwise stated. We say
f\in L^{p}\left({\mathbb{R}},\gamma_{n}\right)
if
f\left(x\right)\in{\mathbb{R}}
for all
x
. We equip
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
with an inner product

\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\gamma_{n}}=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\sim\gamma_{n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\overline{f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)}g\left(\mathbf{x}\right)\right].

Given
p\geq 1
and
f\in L^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
, the
p
-norm
of
f
is defined to be

\left\|f\right\|_{p}=\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}\left|f(x)\right|^{p}\gamma_{n}\left(dx\right)\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Then
\left\langle f,f\right\rangle=\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}
. The set of
Hermite polynomials
forms an orthonormal basis in
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{1}\right)
with respect to the inner product
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle_{\gamma_{1}}
. The Hermite polynomials
H_{r}:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
for
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
are defined as

H_{0}\left(x\right)=1;H_{1}\left(x\right)=x;H_{r}\left(x\right)=\frac{(-1)^{r}}{\sqrt{r!}}e^{x^{2}/2}\frac{d^{r}}{dx^{r}}e^{-x^{2}/2}.

(15)

For any
\sigma\in\left(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{n}\right)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, define

H_{\sigma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
as

H_{\sigma}\left(x\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}H_{\sigma_{i}}\left(x_{i}\right).

(16)

The set
\left\{H_{\sigma}:\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}\right\}
forms an orthonormal basis in
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
. Every function
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
has an
Hermite expansion
as

f\left(x\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\cdot H_{\sigma}\left(x\right),

where
\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)
’s are the
Hermite coefficients
of
f
, which can be obtained by
\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)=\left\langle H_{\sigma},f\right\rangle_{\gamma_{n}}
. Recall that
\mathrm{wt}\left(\sigma\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}
for
\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
. The degree of
f
is defined to be

\deg\left(f\right)=\max\left\{\mathrm{wt}\left(\sigma\right):~\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\neq 0\right\}.

Just as in Fourier analysis, we have
Parseval’s identity
, that is,

\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}}\left|\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}.

We say
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
is
multilinear
if
\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)=0
for
\sigma\notin\left\{0,1\right\}^{n}
.

Definition 2.2
.

Given a function
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
,
the
variance
of
f
is defined to be

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\sim\gamma_{n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)-\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[f\right]\right|^{2}\right].

(17)

For any set
S\subseteq[n]
, the
conditional variance

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)|\mathbf{x}_{S}\right]
is defined to be

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)|\mathbf{x}_{S}\right]=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\sim\gamma_{n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\left|f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)-\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)|\mathbf{x}_{S}\right]\right|^{2}\text{\Large$\mid$}\mathbf{x}_{S}\right].

(18)

The
influence
of the
i
-th coordinate(variable) on
f
, denoted by
\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right)
, is defined by

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right)=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\sim\gamma_{n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\left(\mathbf{x}\right)|\mathbf{x}_{-i}\right]\right].

(19)

The
total influence
of
f
is defined by

\mathrm{Inf}\left(f\right)=\sum_{i}\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right).

The following fact summarizes the basic properties of variance and influence. Readers may refer to
[
66
]
for a thorough treatment.

Fact 2.3
.

[
66
,
62
]

Given
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
, it holds that

1.

\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\in{\mathbb{R}}
if
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}},\gamma_{n}\right)
;

2.

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]=\sum_{\sigma\neq 0^{n}}\left|\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}
;

3.

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right)=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i}\neq 0}\left|\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}
, and hence for all
i
,
\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right)\leq\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]
;

4.

\mathrm{Inf}\left(f\right)=\sum_{\sigma}\left|\sigma\right|\left|\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}
;

5.

\mathrm{Inf}\left(f\right)\leq\deg\left(f\right)\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]
.

Definition 2.4
.

Given
0\leq\rho\leq 1
and
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
, we define the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator
U_{\rho}
to be

U_{\rho}f\left(z\right)=\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}\sim\gamma_{n}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[f\left(\rho z+\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}\mathbf{x}\right)\right].

Fact 2.5
.

[
66
, Page 338, Proposition 11.37]

For any
0\leq\rho\leq 1
and
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
, it holds that

U_{\rho}f=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\rho^{\mathrm{wt}\left(\sigma\right)}H_{\sigma}.

Given
p\geq 1
, a
vector-valued function

f=\left(f_{1},\ldots,f_{k}\right):{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
is in
L^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}}^{k},\gamma_{n}\right)
if
f_{i}\in L^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
for all
i
. It is in
L^{p}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{k},\gamma_{n}\right)
if
f_{i}\in L^{p}\left({\mathbb{R}},\gamma_{n}\right)
for all
i
. For any
f,g\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}^{k},\gamma_{n}\right)
, the inner product of
f
and
g
is defined to be

\left\langle f,g\right\rangle_{\gamma_{n}}=\sum_{t=1}^{k}\left\langle f_{t},g_{t}\right\rangle_{\gamma_{n}}.

The
p
-norm of
f
is

\left\|f\right\|_{p}=\left(\sum_{t=1}^{k}\left\|f_{t}\right\|_{p}^{p}\right)^{1/p}.

For any
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}^{k},\gamma_{n}\right)
, the Hermite coefficients of
f
are the vectors

\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)=\left(\widehat{f_{1}}\left(\sigma\right),\ldots,\widehat{f_{k}}\left(\sigma\right)\right).

The degree of
f
is

\deg\left(f\right)=\max_{t}\deg\left(f_{t}\right).

We say
f
is multilinear if
f_{i}
is multilinear for
1\leq i\leq k
. The variance of
f
is

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]=\sum_{t}\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f_{t}\right].

The influence of the
i
-th coordinate (variable) on
f
is

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right)=\sum_{t}\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f_{t}\right).

The total influence of
f
is

\mathrm{Inf}\left(f\right)=\sum_{i}\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right).

The action of Ornstein-Uhlenbeck operator on
f
is defined to be

U_{\rho}f=\left(U_{\rho}f_{1},\ldots,U_{\rho}f_{k}\right).

For any vector
v\in{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
, the norm of
v
is defined to be

\left\|v\right\|_{2}=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left|v_{i}\right|^{2}}.

2.3
and
2.5
can be directly generalized to vector-valued functions.

Fact 2.6
.

Given
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}}^{k},\gamma_{n}\right)
and
0\leq\rho\leq 1
, it holds that

1.

\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{k}
if
f\in L^{2}\left({\mathbb{R}}^{k},\gamma_{n}\right)
;

2.

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]=\sum_{\sigma\neq 0^{n}}\left\|\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}\leq\left\|f\right\|_{2}^{2}
;

3.

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right)=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i}\neq 0}\left\|\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}
, and hence for all
i
,
\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(f\right)\leq\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]
;

4.

\mathrm{Inf}\left(f\right)=\sum_{\sigma}\left|\sigma\right|\cdot\left\|\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\right\|_{2}^{2}
;

5.

\mathrm{Inf}\left(f\right)\leq\deg\left(f\right)\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[f\right]
;

6.

U_{\rho}f=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{f}\left(\sigma\right)\rho^{\mathrm{wt}\left(\sigma\right)}H_{\sigma}
.

For any
0\leq\rho\leq 1
,
\mathcal{G}_{\rho}
represents a
\rho
-correlated Gaussian distribution, which is a
2
-dimensional Gaussian distribution

\left(X,Y\right)\sim N\left(\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}1&\rho\\
\rho&1\end{pmatrix}\right).

Namely, the marginal distributions
X
and
Y
are distributed according to
\gamma_{1}
and
\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[XY\right]=\rho
.

2.3
Matrix spaces and random matrix spaces

For a matrix
M
, with a slight abuse of notations, we use
M_{i,j}
or
M\left(i,j\right)
to represent the
\left(i,j\right)
-th entry of
M
whichever is convenient.

For
1\leq p\leq\infty
the
p
-norm of
M
is defined to be

\left\|M\right\|_{p}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\min\left\{m,n\right\}}s_{i}\left(M\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p},

where
\left(s_{1}\left(M\right),s_{2}\left(M\right),\ldots\right)
are the singular values of
M
sorted in non-increasing order.
\left\|M\right\|=\left\|M\right\|_{\infty}=s_{1}\left(M\right)
when
p=\infty
. It is easy to verify that
\left\|M\right\|_{p}\leq\left\|M\right\|_{q}
if
p\geq q
. For
m=n
, the
normalized
p
-norm
of
M
is defined as

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p}=\left(\frac{1}{m}\sum_{i=1}^{m}s_{i}\left(M\right)^{p}\right)^{1/p}

(20)

and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\infty}=s_{1}\left(M\right)
. We have
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p}\geq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{q}
if
p\geq q
. For any
M\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
,
\left(\lambda_{1}\left(M\right),\ldots,\lambda_{m}\left(M\right)\right)
represents the eigenvalues of
M
in non-increasing order and
\left|M\right|=\sqrt{M^{\dagger}M}
.

For a Hermitian matrix
X
, suppose it has a spectral decomposition
U^{\dagger}\Lambda U
, where
U
is unitary and
\Lambda
is diagonal. Define

X^{\mathpzc{pos}}=U^{\dagger}\Lambda^{\mathpzc{pos}}U

(21)

where
\Lambda^{\mathpzc{pos}}
is diagonal and
\Lambda^{\mathpzc{pos}}_{ii}=\Lambda_{ii}
if
\Lambda_{ii}\geq 0
, and
\Lambda^{\mathpzc{pos}}_{ii}=0
otherwise. Let
X^{+}
be the
Moore-Penrose inverse
of
X
. Namely,

X^{+}=U^{\dagger}\Lambda^{+}U

(22)

where
\Lambda^{+}
is diagonal and
\Lambda^{+}_{ii}=\Lambda_{ii}^{-1}
if
\Lambda_{ii}\neq 0
, and
\Lambda^{+}_{ii}=0
otherwise.

Given two matrices
A,B
of the same dimension, the
Hadamard product
of
A
and
B
is
A\circ B
, where
\left(A\circ B\right)_{i,j}=A_{i,j}\cdot B_{i,j}
.
The
anticommutator
of
A
and
B
is defined to be
\left\{A,B\right\}=AB+BA
.
Given
P,Q\in\mathcal{M}_{m}
, we define

\left\langle P,Q\right\rangle=\frac{1}{m}\mathrm{Tr}~P^{\dagger}Q.

(23)

Fact 2.7
.

\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
is an inner product.
\left(\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle,\mathcal{M}_{m}\right)
forms a Hilbert space of dimension
m^{2}
. For any
M\in\mathcal{M}_{m}
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}=\left\langle M,M\right\rangle
.

We say
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m^{2}-1}\right\}
is a
standard orthonormal basis
in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
, if it is an orthonormal basis with all elements being Hermitian and
\mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathds{1}_{m}
.

Fact 2.8
.

Given an orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\mathcal{B}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{B}_{m^{2}-1}\right\}
, the set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\left\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{m^{2}-1}\right\}
is also an orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
if and only if there exists an
\left(m^{2}\right)\times\left(m^{2}\right)
unitary matrix
U
with both rows and columns indexed by
[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}
such that

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{m^{2}-1}U_{i,j}\mathcal{B}_{j}

for all

0\leq i\leq m^{2}-1
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{B}
is a standard orthonormal basis, the set
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is also a standard orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
if and only if
U
is an orthogonal matrix and it satisfies
U_{0,j}=\delta_{0,j}
for
0\leq j\leq m^{2}-1
.

Fact 2.9
.

Let
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
be a standard orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
, then

\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}=\otimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{i}}\right\}_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}

is a standard orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
.

The following lemma guarantees the existence of standard orthonormal bases.

Lemma 2.10
.

For any integer
m\geq 2
, there exists a standard orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
.

Proof.

Consider the space
\left(\mathcal{H}_{m},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
i.e., the space of Hermitian matrices over the real number field, with respect to the inner product
\left\langle P,Q\right\rangle=\frac{1}{m}\mathrm{Tr}P^{\dagger}Q
. As the inner product of two Hermitian matrices is real,
\left(\mathcal{H}_{m},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
forms a real Hilbert space. Apparently the dimension of
\left(\mathcal{H}_{m},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
is
m^{2}
and
\mathds{1}_{m}\in\mathcal{H}_{m}
with
\left\langle\mathds{1}_{m},\mathds{1}_{m}\right\rangle=1
. Thus, there exists an orthonormal basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{0\leq i<m^{2}}
in
\left(\mathcal{H}_{m},{\mathbb{R}}\right)
with
\mathcal{B}_{0}=\mathds{1}
. Note that
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{0\leq i<m^{2}}
is still an orthonormal set in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
. Moreover, the dimension of
\left(\mathcal{M}_{m},{\mathbb{C}}\right)
, i.e., the space of
m\times m
matrices over the complex number field, is also
m^{2}
. Thus,
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{0\leq i<m^{2}}
forms a standard orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
.
∎

Given a standard orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
, every matrix
M\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
has a
Fourier expansion
with respect to the basis
\mathcal{B}
given by

M=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

where
\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)
’s are the
Fourier coefficients
of
M
with respect to the basis
\mathcal{B}
. They can be obtained as
\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)=\left\langle\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},M\right\rangle
. The basic properties of
\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)
’s are summarized in the following fact, which can be easily derived from the orthonormality of
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\right\}_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}}
.

Fact 2.11
.

Given a standard orthonormal basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
and
M,N\in\mathcal{M}_{m}
, it holds that

1.

\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)
’s are real for all
\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}
if and only if
M
is Hermitian;

2.

\left\langle M,N\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathds{1},M^{\dagger}N\right\rangle=\left\langle MN^{\dagger},\mathds{1}\right\rangle=\sum_{\sigma}\overline{\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)}\widehat{N}\left(\sigma\right)
;

3.

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|M\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}^{2}=\left\langle M,M\right\rangle=\left\langle M^{\dagger}M,\mathds{1}\right\rangle=\left\langle\mathds{1},M^{\dagger}M\right\rangle=\sum_{\sigma}\left|\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}
;

4.

\left\langle\mathds{1},M\right\rangle=\widehat{M}\left(0\right)
.

The
variance
of a matrix
M\in\mathcal{M}_{m}
is defined to be

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[M\right]=\left\langle M,M\right\rangle-\left\langle M,\mathds{1}\right\rangle\left\langle\mathds{1},M\right\rangle.

The following lemma is easily verified.

Lemma 2.12
.

Given a standard orthonormal basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
and
M\in\mathcal{M}_{m}
, it holds that

\mathrm{Var}\>\!\!\left[M\right]=\sum_{\sigma\neq 0}\left|\widehat{M}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
be a standard orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
,
P,Q\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
and a subset
S\subseteq[n]
.

1.

The degree of
P
is defined to be

\deg P=\max\left\{\left|\sigma\right|:\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\neq 0\right\}.

Recall that
\left|\sigma\right|
represents the number of nonzero entries of
\sigma
.

2.

For any
S\subseteq[n]
,

P_{S}=\frac{1}{m^{|S^{c}|}}\mathrm{Tr}_{S^{c}}P.

3.

For any
S\subseteq[n]
,

\mathrm{Var}_{S}[P]=\left(P^{\dagger}P\right)_{S^{c}}-\left(P_{S^{c}}\right)^{\dagger}\left(P_{S^{c}}\right).

If
S=\left\{i\right\}
, we use
\mathrm{Var}_{i}[P]
in short.

4.

For any
i\in[n]
,

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)=\left\langle\mathds{1},\mathrm{Var}_{i}[P]\right\rangle.

5.

\mathrm{Inf}\left(P\right)=\sum_{i}\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right).

With the notion of degrees, we define the low-degree part and the high-degree part of an operator.

Definition 2.14
.

Given integers
m,t>0
, a standard orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
and
P\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
, we define

P^{\leq t}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}:\left|\sigma\right|\leq t}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma};

P^{\geq t}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}:\left|\sigma\right|\geq t}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}

and

P^{=t}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}:\left|\sigma\right|=t}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma};

where
\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)
’s are the Fourier coefficients of
P
with respect to the basis
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 2.15
.

The degree of
P
is independent of the choices of bases. Moreover,
P^{\leq t},P^{\geq t}
and
P^{=t}
are also independent of the choices of bases.

Proof.

Let
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\right\}_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
and
\left\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\sigma}\right\}_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
be two standard orthonormal bases in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
. From
2.8
, there exists an
\left(m^{2}-1\right)\times\left(m^{2}-1\right)
orthogonal matrix
U
satisfying that

\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}=\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}=1}^{m^{2}-1}U_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\mathcal{B}^{\prime}_{\sigma^{\prime}}

for any
\sigma\in[m^{2}-1]
. Suppose
P=P^{=t}
with respect to the basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\right\}_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
. By linearity, we may assume that
P=\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{i}}
without loss of generality. It is easy to verify that each term in the expansion of
P
in the basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}^{\prime}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
is also of degree
\left|\sigma\right|
. The result follows.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Given
P\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
, a standard orthonormal basis
\mathcal{B}=\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
and a subset
S\subseteq[n]
, it holds that

1.

P_{S}=\left\langle\mathds{1},P\right\rangle_{S^{c}}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{S^{c}}=\mathbf{0}}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{S}}
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{2}
;

2.

\left\langle\mathds{1}_{S^{c}},\mathrm{Var}_{S}[P]\right\rangle=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{S}\neq\mathbf{0}}\left|\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}
;

3.

\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(P\right)=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{i}\neq 0}\left|\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}
;

4.

\mathrm{Inf}\left(P\right)=\sum_{\sigma}\left|\sigma\right|\left|\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}\leq\deg P\cdot{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{2}
.

Proof.

1.

For the equality,

P_{S}=\frac{1}{m^{|S^{c}|}}\sum_{\sigma}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathrm{Tr}_{S^{c}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}=\text{RHS}.

For the inequality,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P_{S}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{2}=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{S^{c}}={\mathbf{0}}}\left|\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}\leq\sum_{\sigma}\left|\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|P\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}^{2}_{2},

where both equalities are from
2.11
item 3.

2.

From item 1,

\left(P^{\dagger}P\right)_{S^{c}}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}\overline{\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)\left(\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime}}\right)_{S^{c}}=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}:\sigma_{S}=\sigma^{\prime}_{S}}\overline{\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{S^{c}}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{S^{c}}}.

Meanwhile,

\left(P_{S^{c}}\right)^{\dagger}\left(P_{S^{c}}\right)=\sum_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}:\sigma_{S}=\sigma^{\prime}_{S}=\mathbf{0}}\overline{\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma^{\prime}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma_{S^{c}}}\mathcal{B}_{\sigma^{\prime}_{S^{c}}}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\left\langle\mathds{1}_{S^{c}},\mathrm{Var}_{S}[P]\right\rangle=\sum_{\sigma:\sigma_{S}\neq\mathbf{0}}\left|\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\right|^{2}.

3.

It follows from item 2 and the definition of
\mathrm{Inf}_{i}\left(\cdot\right)
.

4.

It can be verified by a direct calculation.

∎

Definition 2.17
.

\left(\textbf{Efron-Stein decomposition}\right)
Given integers
n,d>0
, an operator
P\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
, a standard orthonormal basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
and
S\subseteq[n]
, set

P[S]=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{n}:\mathrm{supp}\left(\sigma\right)=S}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

where
\mathrm{supp}\left(\sigma\right)=\left\{i\in[n]:\sigma_{i}>0\right\}
. The Efron-Stein decomposition of
P
is

P=\sum_{S\subseteq[n]}P[S].

Again, the definition of
P[S]
is independent of the choices of the basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
, followed by the same argument for
Lemma

2.15
.

The following proposition can be obtained from the orthogonality of
\mathcal{B}_{i}
’s.

Proposition 2.18
.

Given integers
m,n>0
,
S\neq T\subseteq[n]
and
P,Q\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
, it holds that
\left\langle P[S],Q[T]\right\rangle=0
.

Proposition 2.19
.

Given integers
m,n>0
,
P\in\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
and
S,T\subseteq[n],S\not\subseteq T
, it holds that

\mathrm{Tr}_{T^{c}}~P[S]=0.

Proof.

\mathrm{Tr}_{T^{c}}~P[S]=\mathrm{Tr}_{T^{c}}~\left(\sum_{\sigma:\mathrm{supp}\left(\sigma\right)=S}\widehat{P}\left(\sigma\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma}\right)=0,

where the second equality holds because
S\cap T^{c}\neq\emptyset
.
∎

Lemma 2.20
.

Given
\psi_{AB}
with
\psi_{A}=\frac{\mathds{1}_{m_{A}}}{m_{A}}
and
\psi_{B}=\frac{\mathds{1}_{m_{B}}}{m_{B}}
, where
m_{A}
and
m_{B}
are the dimensions of
A
and
B
, respectively, there exist standard orthonormal bases
\left\{\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in[m_{A}^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
and
\left\{\mathcal{Y}_{\beta}\right\}_{\beta\in[m_{B}^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
in
\mathcal{M}\left(A\right)
and
\mathcal{M}\left(B\right)
, respectively, such that

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\beta}\right)\psi_{AB}\right)=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}c_{\alpha}

for
\alpha\in[m_{A}^{2}]_{\geq 0},\beta\in[m_{B}^{2}]_{\geq 0}
and
c_{\alpha}\geq 0
.

Proof.

Let
\left\{\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha\in[m_{A}^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
and
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{\beta}\right\}_{\beta\in[m_{B}^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
be arbitrary standard orthonormal bases in
\mathcal{M}_{m_{A}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{m_{B}}
, respectively. Let
\left(M_{\alpha,\beta}\right)_{\alpha\in[m_{A}^{2}]_{\geq 0},\beta\in[m_{B}^{2}]_{\geq 0}}
be an
m_{A}^{2}\times m_{B}^{2}
matrix, where

M_{\alpha,\beta}=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{B}_{\beta}\right)\psi_{AB}\right).

Then
M
is a real matrix of the form

M=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&&&&\\
\raisebox{15.0pt}{\vdots}&&\raisebox{15.0pt}{{\huge\mbox{{$M^{\prime}$}}}}&\\
0&&&\end{pmatrix}.

(24)

Let
M^{\prime}=U^{\dagger}DV^{\dagger}
be a singular value decomposition of
M^{\prime}
where
U,V
are both orthogonal matrices and
D
is a diagonal matrix. For any
\alpha\in[m_{A}^{2}]_{\geq 0}
and
\beta\in[m_{B}^{2}]_{\geq 0}
set

\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}=\begin{cases}\sum_{\alpha^{\prime}=1}^{m_{A}^{2}-1}U_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}_{\alpha^{\prime}}~&\mbox{if $\alpha\neq 0$}\\
\mathds{1}_{m_{A}}~&\mbox{otherwise},\end{cases}~\mbox{and}~\mathcal{Y}_{\beta}=\begin{cases}\sum_{\beta^{\prime}=1}^{m_{B}^{2}-1}V_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\mathcal{B}_{\beta^{\prime}}~&\mbox{if $\beta\neq 0$}\\
\mathds{1}_{m_{B}}~&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}.

From
2.8
,
\left\{\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}\right\}_{\alpha=0}^{m_{A}^{2}-1}
and
\left\{\mathcal{Y}_{\beta}\right\}_{\beta=0}^{m_{B}^{2}-1}
are standard orthonormal bases in
\mathcal{M}\left(A\right)
and
\mathcal{M}\left(B\right)
, respectively. Then

\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(\mathcal{X}_{\alpha}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{\beta}\right)\psi_{AB}\right)=\begin{cases}\left(UM^{\prime}V\right)_{\alpha,\beta}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}D_{\alpha,\alpha}~&\mbox{if $\alpha,\beta>0$,}\\
\delta_{\left(0,0\right),\left(\alpha,\beta\right)}~&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

∎

2.4
Random operators

From the previous subsections, we see that the matrix space
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes n}
and Gaussian space
L^{2}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
are both Hilbert spaces. In this subsection, we unify both spaces by random operators.

Definition 2.21
.

Given
p\geq 1
, integers
h,n,m>0
, we say
\mathbf{P}
is a random operator if it can be expressed as

\mathbf{P}=\sum_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}}p_{\sigma}\left(\mathbf{g}\right)\mathcal{B}_{\sigma},

(25)

where
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
is a standard orthonormal basis in
\mathcal{M}_{m}
,
p_{\sigma}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
for all
\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}
and
\mathbf{g}\sim\gamma_{n}.

\mathbf{P}\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes h},\gamma_{n}\right)
if
p_{\sigma}\in L^{p}\left({\mathbb{C}},\gamma_{n}\right)
for all
\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}
. Moreover,
\mathbf{P}\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{H}_{m}^{\otimes n},\gamma_{n}\right)
if
p_{\sigma}\in L^{p}\left({\mathbb{R}},\gamma_{n}\right)
. Define a vector-valued function

p=\left(p_{\sigma}\right)_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}}:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\rightarrow{\mathbb{C}}^{m^{2h}}.

We say
p
is the
associated vector-valued function
of
\mathbf{P}
under the basis
\left\{\mathcal{B}_{i}\right\}_{i=0}^{m^{2}-1}
.

The following is a generalization of the
p
-norm in
L^{2}\left(\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes h},\gamma_{n}\right)
.

Definition 2.22
.
1
1

1

To clarify the potential ambiguity, we consider
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{P}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p}
to be a random variable and use
N_{p}\left(\cdot\right)
to represent the normalized
p
-norm of a random operator.

Given
p\geq 1
, integers
n,h>0,m>1
and random operator
\mathbf{P}\in L^{p}\left(\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\otimes h},\gamma_{n}\right)
, the normalized
p
-norm of
\mathbf{P}
is

N_{p}\left(\mathbf{P}\right)=\left(\bigE_{\begin{subarray}{c}\end{subarray}}\>\!\!\left[{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\mathbf{P}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p}^{p}\right]\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

The degree of
\mathbf{P}
, denoted by
\deg\left(\mathbf{P}\right)
, is

\max_{\sigma\in[m^{2}]_{\geq 0}^{h}}\deg\left(p_{\sigma}\right).

We say
\mathbf{P}
is multilinear if
p_{\sigma}\left(\cdot\right)
